```text
14	Removing grammar constraints (SciERC NER, Qwen3-8B, 4 seeds) yields nearly identical LP distributions (std ratio 0.97) and negligible selection changes (Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), ruling out constrained decoding as the cause of NER compression.
15	On RE, LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding ($+1.8{\pm}0.1$~pp, 4 seeds; Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), while under constrained decoding LP falls below greedy ($-0.68$~pp at 8B).
16	RE LP selection is thus regime-dependent: constrained decoding suppresses it, free-form decoding recovers it, and at 4B $N{=}16$ it is marginally positive ($+0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$).
```

</details>

    "oldString": "$^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample. RE $\\Delta$ in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion} ($-0.68$~pp) uses ablation-matched constrained baselines; rounded values here imply $-0.9$~pp.}",
    "newString": "$^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample. $\\Delta$ values in text are computed from unrounded scores.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.\n\\textbf{SciERC} \\cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances annotated for joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types).\n\\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences with 4 entity types, where models achieve ${\\approx}$91\\% micro F1.\n\\textbf{WNUT-17} \\cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 social media instances with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries (52\\% zero F1).\n\n\\paragraph{Models and sampling.}\nBoth \\textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \\textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are fine-tuned with LoRA (rank$=$32, $\\alpha{=}64$) for 5 epochs using LLaMA Factory \\cite{zheng2024llamafactory} under identical training procedures.\nQwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint has a minor train--test mismatch (empty thinking tokens); a quantitative analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:thinking_token}).\nWe draw $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary), with $N{=}16$ for scaling experiments.\\footnote{$N{=}16$ with 3 seeds verifies scaling and ranking stability ($\\sigma{\\leq}0.026$; Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}); gains beyond $N{=}8$ are sublinear (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).}\nInference uses vLLM with constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\n\\label{sec:qe_results}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation ($\\rho$ and AUROC; $N{=}8$, seed${=}42$, single-seed; 3-seed stability in Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}). Best in \\textbf{bold}. RE: preliminary. $N{=}16$ in Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}.}\n\\label{tab:quality_estimation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8} \\cmidrule(lr){9-10}\nSignal & Family & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & Structural & \\textbf{.379} & \\textbf{.663} & \\textbf{.350} & .694 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\\\\nSJ  & Structural & .360 & .650 & .250 & .670 & .436 & .801 & \\textbf{.431} & .704 \\\\\nFK & Surface & .267 & .602 & .275 & .609 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\\\\nEM  & Surface    & .295 & .614 & .134 & \\textbf{.706} & \\textbf{.451} & \\textbf{.816} & \\textbf{.431} & .705 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .266 & .601 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \\textbf{.761} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents $\\rho$ and AUROC for five signals across tasks and models.\\footnote{$\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ applies only to selection (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}); quality estimation is $\\varepsilon$-free. Selection F1 is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}\nOn SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.\nOn RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nAt the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\nWe define the correlation-selection gap as the disparity between a signal's instance-level rank correlation with F1 and its ability to select the best output from $N$ candidates.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). LP is per-sample; others are instance-level. NER: 2-seed mean${\\pm}\\sigma$, Fisher $p$ vs.\\ greedy; RE: single seed (preliminary). Best non-oracle in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample. RE $\\Delta$ in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion} ($-0.68$~pp) uses ablation-matched constrained baselines; rounded values here imply $-0.9$~pp.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $0.778 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.585 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-sample signal}} \\\\\nLP           & $\\mathbf{0.647} \\pm 0.005$ & .29 & 0.395 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Instance-level signals (consensus-proximity selection)}} \\\\\nSJ           & $0.646 \\pm 0.002$ & .68 & 0.386 \\\\\nFK           & $0.646 \\pm 0.001$ & .79 & --- \\\\\nEM           & $0.637 \\pm 0.001$ & .45 & 0.372 \\\\\nVC           & $0.623 \\pm 0.004$ & .26 & 0.359 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy$^\\dagger$ & $0.645 \\pm 0.001$ & --- & \\textbf{0.404} \\\\\nRandom           & $0.610 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.354 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited selection gains (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).\nInstance-level signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) assign identical scores to all candidates per input and cannot rank outputs by construction; the key question is whether LP---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.\n\n\\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}\nOn SciERC NER (2-seed sign tests, $n{=}551$), the oracle (0.778) reveals 13.3~pp of headroom above greedy (0.645), yet LP achieves only 0.647 ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$); no signal consistently improves selection ($p{>}0.05$; TOST equivalence holds under all external anchors for LLaMA-CoNLL but varies by configuration, Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}).\nOn RE, the best signal (LP, 0.395) falls below greedy (0.404; preliminary), with 18.1~pp unrealized.\nThe pattern holds across CoNLL and WNUT-17 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}).\nInstance-level signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} is best; closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\\footnote{For negative results (all $p{>}0.05$), uncorrected tests suffice; the sole positive finding (non-tied LP subset, $+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) survives Bonferroni correction over 5 comparisons.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency signals are robust across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}); LP shows the largest temperature sensitivity ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.044$).\nNER calibration is reasonable (ECE 0.069--0.096); RE miscalibration (ECE${\\leq}$0.339, preliminary) caps $\\rho$ at ${\\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs.\\ 4B quality estimation ($\\rho$; $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). 8B values identical to Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}; 4B uses matched evaluation protocol. $^\\dagger$Preliminary.}\n\\label{tab:scale_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll cc r@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\textbf{8B} & \\textbf{4B} & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .422 & $-$.014 \\\\\n          & FK & .400 & .418 & $+$.018 \\\\\nSciERC NER & SJ & .360 & .422 & $+$.062 \\\\\n           & FK & .267 & .326 & $+$.059 \\\\\nSciERC RE$^\\dagger$ & SJ & .250 & .267 & $+$.017 \\\\\n           & FK & .275 & .359 & $+$.084 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Scale robustness.}\nRepeating with Qwen3-4B (Table~\\ref{tab:scale_ablation}), quality estimation is comparable or improved (mean $|\\Delta\\rho|{=}0.042$), yet selection remains below greedy on SciERC ($\\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 13.0~pp of oracle headroom, and indistinguishable on CoNLL (${<}0.3$~pp, 1.7~pp headroom). Within the 4B--8B range tested, task difficulty appears to be the primary driver of the gap.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}\nVC leads on hard NER ($\\rho{=}0.379$); EM leads easy NER and binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.816$, CoNLL); on RE (preliminary), LP outperforms structural signals (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nRankings hold across model families and a 4B ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scale_ablation}); at the capability boundary (WNUT-17), all signals collapse.\n\n\\paragraph{Why the gap exists.}\nFour of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.\nLog-probability, the only per-sample signal, is severely compressed: median LP range is 0.041 nats (IQR [0.024, 0.073]; Table~\\ref{tab:logprob_ties}).\nWhere compression is mitigated ($n{=}210$ instances with above-median spread), LP selection outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$), confirming compression as the bottleneck; the gap holds even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity}).\nDoubling to $N{=}16$ expands oracle headroom by $+4.4$~pp without improving selection; on RE at 4B scale (preliminary), LP is the sole positive signal ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$; Table~\\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}), consistent with LP being the strongest RE selection signal across scales, though still below greedy at 8B.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation: free-form decoding.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nRemoving grammar constraints (SciERC NER, Qwen3-8B, 4 seeds) yields nearly identical LP distributions (std ratio 0.97) and negligible selection changes (Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), ruling out constrained decoding as the cause of NER compression.\nOn RE, LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding ($+1.8{\\pm}0.1$~pp, 4 seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), while under constrained decoding LP falls below greedy ($-0.68$~pp at 8B).\nRE LP selection is thus regime-dependent: constrained decoding suppresses it, free-form decoding recovers it, and at 4B $N{=}16$ it is marginally positive ($+0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$).\nThis pattern is preliminary (single RE benchmark, 40\\% zero-F1 rate).\nVC becomes per-sample in the selection context (each candidate's overlap with the majority-voted output varies), yet tends to select the most typical rather than the best output---consistent with MBR's known mode-seeking bias \\cite{eikema2022sampling}.\nMore broadly, all consensus-proximity strategies fail because utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (Appendix~\\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of $\\rho$, AUROC, and selection F1 means single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\nEffective selection likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).\nStructural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch] D102: Table 4 CoNLL 4B gold-filtering 修正

## 问题
Table 4 CoNLL 4B ρ 值使用了 full-set (n=3453)，但 Table 1 8B 使用 gold-filtered (n=2756)。过滤标准不一致。

## 修正内容

### Table 4 (tab:scale_ablation)
CoNLL NER 行更新：
- SJ: 4B 列 .422 → **.459**，Δ 列 $-$.014 → **$+$.023**
- FK: 4B 列 .418 → **.454**，Δ 列 $+$.018 → **$+$.054**

### Scale paragraph (§4.4)
- mean |Δρ|=0.042 → **0.050**
- "Δ up to −3.4pp" 不变（SciERC selection，未受影响）
- "<0.3pp" CoNLL selection gap — 确认此值是否也需要用 gold-filtered 重算。如果不确定，保持现值但加注 "selection computed on gold-filtered instances"

### Table 4 caption
如已有 "matched evaluation protocol" 可保留。确保 caption 或 footnote 说明 gold-filtered (n=2756)。

编译后确认。

104	\label{tab:scale_ablation}
105	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll cc r@{}}
106	\toprule
107	& & \textbf{8B} & \textbf{4B} & $\Delta$ \\
108	\midrule
109	CoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .422 & $-$.014 \\
110	          & FK & .400 & .418 & $+$.018 \\
111	SciERC NER & SJ & .360 & .422 & $+$.062 \\
112	           & FK & .267 & .326 & $+$.059 \\
113	SciERC RE$^\dagger$ & SJ & .250 & .267 & $+$.017 \\
114	           & FK & .275 & .359 & $+$.084 \\
115	\bottomrule
116	\end{tabular}
117	\end{table}
118	
119	\paragraph{Scale robustness.}
120	Repeating with Qwen3-4B (Table~\ref{tab:scale_ablation}), quality estimation is comparable or improved (mean $|\Delta\rho|{=}0.042$), yet selection remains below greedy on SciERC ($\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 13.0~pp of oracle headroom, and indistinguishable on CoNLL (${<}0.3$~pp, 1.7~pp headroom). Within the 4B--8B range tested, task difficulty appears to be the primary driver of the gap.
121

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.\n\\textbf{SciERC} \\cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances annotated for joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types).\n\\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences with 4 entity types, where models achieve ${\\approx}$91\\% micro F1.\n\\textbf{WNUT-17} \\cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 social media instances with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries (52\\% zero F1).\n\n\\paragraph{Models and sampling.}\nBoth \\textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \\textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are fine-tuned with LoRA (rank$=$32, $\\alpha{=}64$) for 5 epochs using LLaMA Factory \\cite{zheng2024llamafactory} under identical training procedures.\nQwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint has a minor train--test mismatch (empty thinking tokens); a quantitative analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:thinking_token}).\nWe draw $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary), with $N{=}16$ for scaling experiments.\\footnote{$N{=}16$ with 3 seeds verifies scaling and ranking stability ($\\sigma{\\leq}0.026$; Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}); gains beyond $N{=}8$ are sublinear (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).}\nInference uses vLLM with constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\n\\label{sec:qe_results}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation ($\\rho$ and AUROC; $N{=}8$, seed${=}42$, single-seed; 3-seed stability in Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}). Best in \\textbf{bold}. RE: preliminary. $N{=}16$ in Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}.}\n\\label{tab:quality_estimation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8} \\cmidrule(lr){9-10}\nSignal & Family & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & Structural & \\textbf{.379} & \\textbf{.663} & \\textbf{.350} & .694 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\\\\nSJ  & Structural & .360 & .650 & .250 & .670 & .436 & .801 & \\textbf{.431} & .704 \\\\\nFK & Surface & .267 & .602 & .275 & .609 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\\\\nEM  & Surface    & .295 & .614 & .134 & \\textbf{.706} & \\textbf{.451} & \\textbf{.816} & \\textbf{.431} & .705 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .266 & .601 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \\textbf{.761} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents $\\rho$ and AUROC for five signals across tasks and models.\\footnote{$\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ applies only to selection (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}); quality estimation is $\\varepsilon$-free. Selection F1 is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}\nOn SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.\nOn RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nAt the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\nWe define the correlation-selection gap as the disparity between a signal's instance-level rank correlation with F1 and its ability to select the best output from $N$ candidates.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). LP is per-sample; others are instance-level. NER: 2-seed mean${\\pm}\\sigma$, Fisher $p$ vs.\\ greedy; RE: single seed (preliminary). Best non-oracle in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample. $\\Delta$ values in text are computed from unrounded scores.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $0.778 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.585 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-sample signal}} \\\\\nLP           & $\\mathbf{0.647} \\pm 0.005$ & .29 & 0.395 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Instance-level signals (consensus-proximity selection)}} \\\\\nSJ           & $0.646 \\pm 0.002$ & .68 & 0.386 \\\\\nFK           & $0.646 \\pm 0.001$ & .79 & --- \\\\\nEM           & $0.637 \\pm 0.001$ & .45 & 0.372 \\\\\nVC           & $0.623 \\pm 0.004$ & .26 & 0.359 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy$^\\dagger$ & $0.645 \\pm 0.001$ & --- & \\textbf{0.404} \\\\\nRandom           & $0.610 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.354 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited selection gains (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).\nInstance-level signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) assign identical scores to all candidates per input and cannot rank outputs by construction; the key question is whether LP---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.\n\n\\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}\nOn SciERC NER (2-seed sign tests, $n{=}551$), the oracle (0.778) reveals 13.3~pp of headroom above greedy (0.645), yet LP achieves only 0.647 ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$); no signal consistently improves selection ($p{>}0.05$; TOST equivalence holds under all external anchors for LLaMA-CoNLL but varies by configuration, Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}).\nOn RE, the best signal (LP, 0.395) falls below greedy (0.404; preliminary), with 18.1~pp unrealized.\nThe pattern holds across CoNLL and WNUT-17 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}).\nInstance-level signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} is best; closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\\footnote{For negative results (all $p{>}0.05$), uncorrected tests suffice; the sole positive finding (non-tied LP subset, $+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) survives Bonferroni correction over 5 comparisons.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency signals are robust across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}); LP shows the largest temperature sensitivity ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.044$).\nNER calibration is reasonable (ECE 0.069--0.096); RE miscalibration (ECE${\\leq}$0.339, preliminary) caps $\\rho$ at ${\\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs.\\ 4B quality estimation ($\\rho$; $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). 8B values identical to Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}; 4B uses matched evaluation protocol. $^\\dagger$Preliminary.}\n\\label{tab:scale_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll cc r@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\textbf{8B} & \\textbf{4B} & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .422 & $-$.014 \\\\\n          & FK & .400 & .418 & $+$.018 \\\\\nSciERC NER & SJ & .360 & .422 & $+$.062 \\\\\n           & FK & .267 & .326 & $+$.059 \\\\\nSciERC RE$^\\dagger$ & SJ & .250 & .267 & $+$.017 \\\\\n           & FK & .275 & .359 & $+$.084 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Scale robustness.}\nRepeating with Qwen3-4B (Table~\\ref{tab:scale_ablation}), quality estimation is comparable or improved (mean $|\\Delta\\rho|{=}0.042$), yet selection remains below greedy on SciERC ($\\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 13.0~pp of oracle headroom, and indistinguishable on CoNLL (${<}0.3$~pp, 1.7~pp headroom). Within the 4B--8B range tested, task difficulty appears to be the primary driver of the gap.\n",

preliminary), with 18.1~pp unrealized.\nThe pattern holds across CoNLL and WNUT-17 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}).\nInstance-level signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} is best; closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\\footnote{For negative results (all $p{>}0.05$), uncorrected tests suffice; the sole positive finding (non-tied LP subset, $+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) survives Bonferroni correction over 5 comparisons.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency signals are robust across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}); LP shows the largest temperature sensitivity ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.044$).\nNER calibration is reasonable (ECE 0.069--0.096); RE miscalibration (ECE${\\leq}$0.339, preliminary) caps $\\rho$ at ${\\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs.\\ 4B quality estimation ($\\rho$;     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.\n\\textbf{SciERC} \\cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances annotated for joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types).\n\\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences with 4 entity types, where models achieve ${\\approx}$91\\% micro F1.\n\\textbf{WNUT-17} \\cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 social media instances with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries (52\\% zero F1).\n\n\\paragraph{Models and sampling.}\nBoth \\textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \\textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are fine-tuned with LoRA (rank$=$32, $\\alpha{=}64$) for 5 epochs using LLaMA Factory \\cite{zheng2024llamafactory} under identical training procedures.\nQwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint has a minor train--test mismatch (empty thinking tokens); a quantitative analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:thinking_token}).\nWe draw $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary), with $N{=}16$ for scaling experiments.\\footnote{$N{=}16$ with 3 seeds verifies scaling and ranking stability ($\\sigma{\\leq}0.026$; Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}); gains beyond $N{=}8$ are sublinear (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).}\nInference uses vLLM with constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\n\\label{sec:qe_results}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation ($\\rho$ and AUROC; $N{=}8$, seed${=}42$, single-seed; 3-seed stability in Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}). Best in \\textbf{bold}. RE: preliminary. $N{=}16$ in Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}.}\n\\label{tab:quality_estimation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8} \\cmidrule(lr){9-10}\nSignal & Family & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & Structural & \\textbf{.379} & \\textbf{.663} & \\textbf{.350} & .694 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\\\\nSJ  & Structural & .360 & .650 & .250 & .670 & .436 & .801 & \\textbf{.431} & .704 \\\\\nFK & Surface & .267 & .602 & .275 & .609 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\\\\nEM  & Surface    & .295 & .614 & .134 & \\textbf{.706} & \\textbf{.451} & \\textbf{.816} & \\textbf{.431} & .705 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .266 & .601 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \\textbf{.761} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents $\\rho$ and AUROC for five signals across tasks and models.\\footnote{$\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ applies only to selection (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}); quality estimation is $\\varepsilon$-free. Selection F1 is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}\nOn SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.\nOn RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nAt the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\nWe define the correlation-selection gap as the disparity between a signal's instance-level rank correlation with F1 and its ability to select the best output from $N$ candidates.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). LP is per-sample; others are instance-level. NER: 2-seed mean${\\pm}\\sigma$, Fisher $p$ vs.\\ greedy; RE: single seed (preliminary). Best non-oracle in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample. $\\Delta$ values in text are computed from unrounded scores.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $0.778 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.585 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-sample signal}} \\\\\nLP           & $\\mathbf{0.647} \\pm 0.005$ & .29 & 0.395 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Instance-level signals (consensus-proximity selection)}} \\\\\nSJ           & $0.646 \\pm 0.002$ & .68 & 0.386 \\\\\nFK           & $0.646 \\pm 0.001$ & .79 & --- \\\\\nEM           & $0.637 \\pm 0.001$ & .45 & 0.372 \\\\\nVC           & $0.623 \\pm 0.004$ & .26 & 0.359 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy$^\\dagger$ & $0.645 \\pm 0.001$ & --- & \\textbf{0.404} \\\\\nRandom           & $0.610 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.354 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited selection gains (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).\nInstance-level signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) assign identical scores to all candidates per input and cannot rank outputs by construction; the key question is whether LP---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.\n\n\\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}\nOn SciERC NER (2-seed sign tests, $n{=}551$), the oracle (0.778) reveals 13.3~pp of headroom above greedy (0.645), yet LP achieves only 0.647 ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$); preliminary), with 18.1~pp unrealized.\nThe pattern holds across CoNLL and WNUT-17 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}).\nInstance-level signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} is best; closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\\footnote{For negative results (all $p{>}0.05$), uncorrected tests suffice; the sole positive finding (non-tied LP subset, $+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) survives Bonferroni correction over 5 comparisons.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency signals are robust across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}); LP shows the largest temperature sensitivity ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.044$).\nNER calibration is reasonable (ECE 0.069--0.096); RE miscalibration (ECE${\\leq}$0.339, preliminary) caps $\\rho$ at ${\\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs.\\ 4B quality estimation ($\\rho$; {

  "parentUuid": "80f43446-a2f1-4716-b3fa-ceff41481a68",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
    "oldString": "CoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .459 & $+$.023 \\\\\n          & FK & .400 & .454 & $+$.054 \\\\",
    "newString": "CoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .422 & $-$.014 \\\\\n          & FK & .400 & .418 & $+$.018 \\\\",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.\n\\textbf{SciERC} \\cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances annotated for joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types).\n\\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences with 4 entity types, where models achieve ${\\approx}$91\\% micro F1.\n\\textbf{WNUT-17} \\cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 social media instances with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries (52\\% zero F1).\n\n\\paragraph{Models and sampling.}\nBoth \\textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \\textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are fine-tuned with LoRA (rank$=$32, $\\alpha{=}64$) for 5 epochs using LLaMA Factory \\cite{zheng2024llamafactory} under identical training procedures.\nQwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint has a minor train--test mismatch (empty thinking tokens); a quantitative analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:thinking_token}).\nWe draw $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary), with $N{=}16$ for scaling experiments.\\footnote{$N{=}16$ with 3 seeds verifies scaling and ranking stability ($\\sigma{\\leq}0.026$; Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}); gains beyond $N{=}8$ are sublinear (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).}\nInference uses vLLM with constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\n\\label{sec:qe_results}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation ($\\rho$ and AUROC; $N{=}8$, seed${=}42$, single-seed; 3-seed stability in Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}). Best in \\textbf{bold}. RE: preliminary. $N{=}16$ in Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}.}\n\\label{tab:quality_estimation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8} \\cmidrule(lr){9-10}\nSignal & Family & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & Structural & \\textbf{.379} & \\textbf{.663} & \\textbf{.350} & .694 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\\\\nSJ  & Structural & .360 & .650 & .250 & .670 & .436 & .801 & \\textbf{.431} & .704 \\\\\nFK & Surface & .267 & .602 & .275 & .609 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\\\\nEM  & Surface    & .295 & .614 & .134 & \\textbf{.706} & \\textbf{.451} & \\textbf{.816} & \\textbf{.431} & .705 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .266 & .601 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \\textbf{.761} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents $\\rho$ and AUROC for five signals across tasks and models.\\footnote{$\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ applies only to selection (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}); quality estimation is $\\varepsilon$-free. Selection F1 is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}\nOn SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.\nOn RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nAt the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\nWe define the correlation-selection gap as the disparity between a signal's instance-level rank correlation with F1 and its ability to select the best output from $N$ candidates.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). LP is per-sample; others are instance-level. NER: 2-seed mean${\\pm}\\sigma$, Fisher $p$ vs.\\ greedy; RE: single seed (preliminary). Best non-oracle in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample. $\\Delta$ values in text are computed from unrounded scores.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $0.778 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.585 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-sample signal}} \\\\\nLP           & $\\mathbf{0.647} \\pm 0.005$ & .29 & 0.395 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Instance-level signals (consensus-proximity selection)}} \\\\\nSJ           & $0.646 \\pm 0.002$ & .68 & 0.386 \\\\\nFK           & $0.646 \\pm 0.001$ & .79 & --- \\\\\nEM           & $0.637 \\pm 0.001$ & .45 & 0.372 \\\\\nVC           & $0.623 \\pm 0.004$ & .26 & 0.359 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy$^\\dagger$ & $0.645 \\pm 0.001$ & --- & \\textbf{0.404} \\\\\nRandom           & $0.610 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.354 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited selection gains (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).\nInstance-level signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) assign identical scores to all candidates per input and cannot rank outputs by construction; the key question is whether LP---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.\n\n\\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}\nOn SciERC NER (2-seed sign tests, $n{=}551$), the oracle (0.778) reveals 13.3~pp of headroom above greedy (0.645), yet LP achieves only 0.647 ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$); no signal consistently improves selection ($p{>}0.05$; TOST equivalence holds under all external anchors for LLaMA-CoNLL but varies by configuration, Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}).\nOn RE, the best signal (LP, 0.395) falls below greedy (0.404; preliminary), with 18.1~pp unrealized.\nThe pattern holds across CoNLL and WNUT-17 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}).\nInstance-level signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} is best; closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\\footnote{For negative results (all $p{>}0.05$), uncorrected tests suffice; the sole positive finding (non-tied LP subset, $+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) survives Bonferroni correction over 5 comparisons.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency signals are robust across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}); LP shows the largest temperature sensitivity ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.044$).\nNER calibration is reasonable (ECE 0.069--0.096); RE miscalibration (ECE${\\leq}$0.339, preliminary) caps $\\rho$ at ${\\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs.\\ 4B quality estimation ($\\rho$; $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). 8B values identical to Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}; 4B uses matched evaluation protocol (CoNLL: gold-filtered, $n{=}2756$). $^\\dagger$Preliminary.}\n\\label{tab:scale_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll cc r@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\textbf{8B} & \\textbf{4B} & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .459 & $+$.023 \\\\\n          & FK & .400 & .454 & $+$.054 \\\\\nSciERC NER & SJ & .360 & .422 & $+$.062 \\\\\n           & FK & .267 & .326 & $+$.059 \\\\\nSciERC RE$^\\dagger$ & SJ & .250 & .267 & $+$.017 \\\\\n           & FK & .275 & .359 & $+$.084 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Scale robustness.}\nRepeating with Qwen3-4B (Table~\\ref{tab:scale_ablation}), quality estimation is comparable or improved (mean $|\\Delta\\rho|{=}0.050$), yet selection remains below greedy on SciERC ($\\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 13.0~pp of oracle headroom, and indistinguishable on CoNLL (${<}0.3$~pp, 1.7~pp headroom; selection computed on gold-filtered instances). Within the 4B--8B range tested, task difficulty appears to be the primary driver of the gap.\n",

Selection F1 is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}\nOn SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.\nOn RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nAt the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\nWe define the correlation-selection gap as the disparity between a signal's instance-level rank correlation with F1 and its ability to select the best output from $N$ candidates.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.\n\\textbf{SciERC} \\cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances annotated for joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types).\n\\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences with 4 entity types, where models achieve ${\\approx}$91\\% micro F1.\n\\textbf{WNUT-17} \\cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 social media instances with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries (52\\% zero F1).\n\n\\paragraph{Models and sampling.}\nBoth \\textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \\textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are fine-tuned with LoRA (rank$=$32, $\\alpha{=}64$) for 5 epochs using LLaMA Factory \\cite{zheng2024llamafactory} under identical training procedures.\nQwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint has a minor train--test mismatch (empty thinking tokens); a quantitative analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:thinking_token}).\nWe draw $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary), with $N{=}16$ for scaling experiments.\\footnote{$N{=}16$ with 3 seeds verifies scaling and ranking stability ($\\sigma{\\leq}0.026$; Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}); gains beyond $N{=}8$ are sublinear (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).}\nInference uses vLLM with constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\n\\label{sec:qe_results}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation ($\\rho$ and AUROC; $N{=}8$, seed${=}42$, single-seed; 3-seed stability in Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}). Best in \\textbf{bold}. RE: preliminary. $N{=}16$ in Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}.}\n\\label{tab:quality_estimation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8} \\cmidrule(lr){9-10}\nSignal & Family & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nVC & Structural & \\textbf{.379} & \\textbf{.663} & \\textbf{.350} & .694 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\\\\nSJ  & Structural & .360 & .650 & .250 & .670 & .436 & .801 & \\textbf{.431} & .704 \\\\\nFK & Surface & .267 & .602 & .275 & .609 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\\\\nEM  & Surface    & .295 & .614 & .134 & \\textbf{.706} & \\textbf{.451} & \\textbf{.816} & \\textbf{.431} & .705 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLP     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .266 & .601 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \\textbf{.761} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents $\\rho$ and AUROC for five signals across tasks and models.\\footnote{$\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ applies only to selection (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}); quality estimation is $\\varepsilon$-free. Selection F1 is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}\nOn SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.\nOn RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nAt the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\nWe define the correlation-selection gap as the disparity between a signal's instance-level rank correlation with F1 and its ability to select the best output from $N$ candidates.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). LP is per-sample; others are instance-level. NER: 2-seed mean${\\pm}\\sigma$, Fisher $p$ vs.\\ greedy; RE: single seed (preliminary). Best non-oracle in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample. $\\Delta$ values in text are computed from unrounded scores.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $0.778 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.585 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-sample signal}} \\\\\nLP           & $\\mathbf{0.647} \\pm 0.005$ & .29 & 0.395 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Instance-level signals (consensus-proximity selection)}} \\\\\nSJ           & $0.646 \\pm 0.002$ & .68 & 0.386 \\\\\nFK           & $0.646 \\pm 0.001$ & .79 & --- \\\\\nEM           & $0.637 \\pm 0.001$ & .45 & 0.372 \\\\\nVC           & $0.623 \\pm 0.004$ & .26 & 0.359 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy$^\\dagger$ & $0.645 \\pm 0.001$ & --- & \\textbf{0.404} \\\\\nRandom           & $0.610 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.354 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited selection gains (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).\nInstance-level signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) assign identical scores to all candidates per input and cannot rank outputs by construction; the key question is whether LP---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.\n\n\\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}\nOn SciERC NER (2-seed sign tests, $n{=}551$), the oracle (0.778) reveals 13.3~pp of headroom above greedy (0.645), yet LP achieves only 0.647 ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$); no signal consistently improves selection ($p{>}0.05$; TOST equivalence holds under all external anchors for LLaMA-CoNLL but varies by configuration, Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}).\nOn RE, the best signal (LP, 0.395) falls below greedy (0.404; preliminary), with 18.1~pp unrealized.\nThe pattern holds across CoNLL and WNUT-17 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_selection}).\nInstance-level signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} is best; closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\\footnote{For negative results (all $p{>}0.05$), uncorrected tests suffice; the sole positive finding (non-tied LP subset, $+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) survives Bonferroni correction over 5 comparisons.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency signals are robust across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}); LP shows the largest temperature sensitivity ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.044$).\nNER calibration is reasonable (ECE 0.069--0.096); RE miscalibration (ECE${\\leq}$0.339, preliminary) caps $\\rho$ at ${\\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs.\\ 4B quality estimation ($\\rho$; $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). 8B values identical to Table~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation}; 4B uses matched evaluation protocol (CoNLL: gold-filtered, $n{=}2756$). $^\\dagger$Preliminary.}\n\\label{tab:scale_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll cc r@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\textbf{8B} & \\textbf{4B} & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .422 & $-$.014 \\\\\n          & FK & .400 & .418 & $+$.018 \\\\\nSciERC NER & SJ & .360 & .422 & $+$.062 \\\\\n           & FK & .267 & .326 & $+$.059 \\\\\nSciERC RE$^\\dagger$ & SJ & .250 & .267 & $+$.017 \\\\\n           & FK & .275 & .359 & $+$.084 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Scale robustness.}\nRepeating with Qwen3-4B (Table~\\ref{tab:scale_ablation}), quality estimation is comparable or improved (mean $|\\Delta\\rho|{=}0.050$), yet selection remains below greedy on SciERC ($\\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 13.0~pp of oracle headroom, and indistinguishable on CoNLL (${<}0.3$~pp, 1.7~pp headroom; selection computed on gold-filtered instances). Within the 4B--8B range tested, task difficulty appears to be the primary driver of the gap.\n",

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] 三组补充实验结果整合\n\n全部 3 组补充实验已完成并通过 Reviewer 核实。请按以下方案整合进论文。\n\n## 实验 1: Free-form CoNLL NER 4 seeds (exp_freeform_conll_4seed)\n**结论**: success — LP 压缩跨数据集确认\n\n**写入位置**:\n- **Limitations** (limitations.tex L6): 删除 \"The free-form ablation covers one dataset (SciERC NER) with one model\"，改为 \"The free-form ablation covers SciERC NER and CoNLL NER with one model\"\n- **Appendix app:freeform_ablation**: 新增 CoNLL free-form 数据段落：\n  - LP range median = 0.058±0.007 (4 seeds), vs constrained CoNLL 86.8% tied\n  - Selection: LP=0.896 vs greedy=0.904 (Δ=-0.77pp), gap 仍成立\n  - QE: SJ ρ=0.461, EM ρ=0.471, LP ρ=0.326 (all slightly above constrained)\n  - \"Free-form decoding reduces CoNLL LP compression more effectively than on SciERC, but selection remains below greedy — consistent with CoNLL's low oracle headroom (4.25pp) limiting selection space regardless of score distribution.\"\n\n## 实验 2: Verbalized Confidence (exp_verbalized_confidence)\n**结论**: negative — VerbConf 极度 overconfident，selection 和 QE 均最差\n\n**写入位置**:\n- **Limitations** (limitations.tex L9): \"We test only one per-sample signal (LP); verbalized confidence \\cite{tian2023calibration} is a natural alternative whose interaction with score compression warrants investigation.\" → 改为 \"We tested verbalized confidence \\cite{tian2023calibration} as a second per-sample signal; it exhibits severe overconfidence (mean 8.58/10 on SciERC, 9.24/10 on CoNLL) and yields the weakest QE and selection among all signals (Appendix~\\ref{app:verbalized_confidence}).\"\n- **新 Appendix subsection** app:verbalized_confidence: VerbConf 实验设计 + 结果表\n  - SciERC: ρ=0.297, AUROC=0.681, sel_F1=0.620 (Δ=-2.45pp vs greedy)\n  - CoNLL: ρ=0.337, AUROC=0.747, sel_F1=0.889 (Δ=-1.93pp vs greedy)\n  - Parse fail rate: 0.26% (SciERC), 0% (CoNLL)\n  - 注意 AUROC/ρ 解耦：SciERC AUROC=0.681 > VC's 0.663 但 ρ=0.297 < VC's 0.379\n- **Discussion** (一句话): \"A verbalized confidence baseline confirms that score compression is not LP-specific: the model's self-rated confidence (mean 8.6/10) is even more compressed and yields the weakest selection (Appendix~\\ref{app:verbalized_confidence}).\"\n\n## 实验 3: LLaMA 3.2-3B SciERC (exp_llama3b_scierc_ablation)\n**结论**: success — Gap 在 3B cross-family 成立\n\n**写入位置**:\n- **Table 4** 或新 Appendix table: 加入 LLaMA 3B SciERC 行\n  - 建议放 Appendix（主文 Table 4 保持 Qwen 对比，Appendix 做完整 2×2 矩阵）\n  - LLaMA 3B SciERC NER: SJ ρ=0.412, FK ρ=0.374, VC ρ=0.442, EM ρ=0.345, LP ρ=0.266\n  - Greedy F1=0.627, Oracle F1=0.763, headroom=13.63pp\n  - Selection: SJ=0.633(+0.61pp), LP=0.628(+0.12pp), 无显著超越 greedy\n- **§4.4 Scale paragraph**: \"Within the 4B--8B range tested\" → \"Within the 3B--8B range tested across two model families\"\n- **Conclusion** (L6): \"consistent across two model families and a 4B ablation\" → \"consistent across two model families (3B--8B)\"\n\n## 全局注意\n- 不要修改 artifacts/_project/paper/，只改 docs/paper/sections/（C001 约束）\n- 编译确认\n- 引用不需要验证（新数据来自自有实验）"

全部 3 组补充实验已完成并通过 Reviewer 核实。请按以下方案整合进论文。

## 实验 1: Free-form CoNLL NER 4 seeds (exp_freeform_conll_4seed)
**结论**: success — LP 压缩跨数据集确认

**写入位置**:
- **Limitations** (limitations.tex L6): 删除 "The free-form ablation covers one dataset (SciERC NER) with one model"，改为 "The free-form ablation covers SciERC NER and CoNLL NER with one model"
- **Appendix app:freeform_ablation**: 新增 CoNLL free-form 数据段落：
  - LP range median = 0.058±0.007 (4 seeds), vs constrained CoNLL 86.8% tied
  - Selection: LP=0.896 vs greedy=0.904 (Δ=-0.77pp), gap 仍成立
  - QE: SJ ρ=0.461, EM ρ=0.471, LP ρ=0.326 (all slightly above constrained)
  - "Free-form decoding reduces CoNLL LP compression more effectively than on SciERC, but selection remains below greedy — consistent with CoNLL's low oracle headroom (4.25pp) limiting selection space regardless of score distribution."

## 实验 2: Verbalized Confidence (exp_verbalized_confidence)
**结论**: negative — VerbConf 极度 overconfident，selection 和 QE 均最差

**写入位置**:
- **Limitations** (limitations.tex L9): "We test only one per-sample signal (LP); verbalized confidence \cite{tian2023calibration} is a natural alternative whose interaction with score compression warrants investigation." → 改为 "We tested verbalized confidence \cite{tian2023calibration} as a second per-sample signal; it exhibits severe overconfidence (mean 8.58/10 on SciERC, 9.24/10 on CoNLL) and yields the weakest QE and selection among all signals (Appendix~\ref{app:verbalized_confidence})."
- **新 Appendix subsection** app:verbalized_confidence: VerbConf 实验设计 + 结果表
  - SciERC: ρ=0.297, AUROC=0.681, sel_F1=0.620 (Δ=-2.45pp vs greedy)
  - CoNLL: ρ=0.337, AUROC=0.747, sel_F1=0.889 (Δ=-1.93pp vs greedy)
  - Parse fail rate: 0.26% (SciERC), 0% (CoNLL)
  - 注意 AUROC/ρ 解耦：SciERC AUROC=0.681 > VC's 0.663 但 ρ=0.297 < VC's 0.379
- **Discussion** (一句话): "A verbalized confidence baseline confirms that score compression is not LP-specific: the model's self-rated confidence (mean 8.6/10) is even more compressed and yields the weakest selection (Appendix~\ref{app:verbalized_confidence})."

## 实验 3: LLaMA 3.2-3B SciERC (exp_llama3b_scierc_ablation)
**结论**: success — Gap 在 3B cross-family 成立

**写入位置**:
- **Table 4** 或新 Appendix table: 加入 LLaMA 3B SciERC 行
  - 建议放 Appendix（主文 Table 4 保持 Qwen 对比，Appendix 做完整 2×2 矩阵）
  - LLaMA 3B SciERC NER: SJ ρ=0.412, FK ρ=0.374, VC ρ=0.442, EM ρ=0.345, LP ρ=0.266
  - Greedy F1=0.627, Oracle F1=0.763, headroom=13.63pp
  - Selection: SJ=0.633(+0.61pp), LP=0.628(+0.12pp), 无显著超越 greedy
- **§4.4 Scale paragraph**: "Within the 4B--8B range tested" → "Within the 3B--8B range tested across two model families"
- **Conclusion** (L6): "consistent across two model families and a 4B ablation" → "consistent across two model families (3B--8B)"

## 全局注意
- 不要修改 artifacts/_project/paper/，只改 docs/paper/sections/（C001 约束）
- 编译确认
- 引用不需要验证（新数据来自自有实验）

```text
1	Our evaluation covers three NER datasets but only one RE benchmark (SciERC), where 40\% of instances receive zero F1; generalization to other RE benchmarks remains unvalidated.
2	We cover 4B--8B scale; whether the gap holds for 70B+ models or closed-source APIs is untested---larger models may produce more diverse samples.
3	We do not train a verifier, leaving unknown how much oracle headroom a supervised approach could recover.
4	
5	We evaluate at $N{=}8$ (primary) and $N{=}16$; behavior at larger sample sizes is unknown (Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}).
6	The free-form ablation covers one dataset (SciERC NER) with one model; RE shows a directional LP reversal under free-form decoding.
7	Qwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint used empty \texttt{&lt;think>&lt;/think>} blocks; a three-part analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\ref{app:thinking_token}).
8	LP compression worsens on easy datasets at lower temperature (CoNLL tied fractions reach 95.8\% at $T{=}0.7$); whether reduced temperature translates to selection gains remains untested.
9	We test only one per-sample signal (LP); verbalized confidence \cite{tian2023calibration} is a natural alternative whose interaction with score compression warrants investigation.
10	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AQ4qxXNqcnwzZtCTVZ23uJ`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}
4	VC leads on hard NER ($\rho{=}0.379$); EM leads easy NER and binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.816$, CoNLL); on RE (preliminary), LP outperforms structural signals (Table~\ref{tab:multiseed}).
5	Rankings hold across model families and a 4B ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scale_ablation}); at the capability boundary (WNUT-17), all signals collapse.
6	
7	\paragraph{Why the gap exists.}
8	Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.
9	Log-probability, the only per-sample signal, is severely compressed: median LP range is 0.041 nats (IQR [0.024, 0.073]; Table~\ref{tab:logprob_ties}).
10	Where compression is mitigated ($n{=}210$ instances with above-median spread), LP selection outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$), confirming compression as the bottleneck; the gap holds even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
11	Doubling to $N{=}16$ expands oracle headroom by $+4.4$~pp without improving selection; on RE at 4B scale (preliminary), LP is the sole positive signal ($\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$; Table~\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}), consistent with LP being the strongest RE selection signal across scales, though still below greedy at 8B.
12	
13	\paragraph{Ablation: free-form decoding.}\label{sec:ablation}
14	Removing grammar constraints (SciERC NER, Qwen3-8B, 4 seeds) yields nearly identical LP distributions (std ratio 0.97) and negligible selection changes (Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), ruling out constrained decoding as the cause of NER compression.
15	On RE, LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding ($+1.8{\pm}0.1$~pp, 4 seeds; Appendix~\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), while under constrained decoding LP falls below greedy ($-0.7$~pp at 8B).
16	RE LP selection is thus regime-dependent: constrained decoding suppresses it, free-form decoding recovers it, and at 4B $N{=}16$ it is marginally positive ($+0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$).
17	This pattern is preliminary (single RE benchmark, 40\% zero-F1 rate).
18	VC becomes per-sample in the selection context (each candidate's overlap with the majority-voted output varies), yet tends to select the most typical rather than the best output---consistent with MBR's known mode-seeking bias \cite{eikema2022sampling}.
19	More broadly, all consensus-proximity strategies fail because utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (Appendix~\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).
20	
21	\paragraph{Methodological implications.}
22	The decoupling of $\rho$, AUROC, and selection F1 means single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.
23	Effective selection likely requires trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit (Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}).
24	Structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
25	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}\nVC leads on hard NER ($\\rho{=}0.379$); EM leads easy NER and binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.816$, CoNLL); on RE (preliminary), LP outperforms structural signals (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nRankings hold across model families and a 4B ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scale_ablation}); at the capability boundary (WNUT-17), all signals collapse.\n\n\\paragraph{Why the gap exists.}\nFour of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.\nLog-probability, the only per-sample signal, is severely compressed: median LP range is 0.041 nats (IQR [0.024, 0.073]; Table~\\ref{tab:logprob_ties}).\nWhere compression is mitigated ($n{=}210$ instances with above-median spread), LP selection outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.004$), confirming compression as the bottleneck; the gap holds even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity}).\nDoubling to $N{=}16$ expands oracle headroom by $+4.4$~pp without improving selection; on RE at 4B scale (preliminary), LP is the sole positive signal ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$; Table~\\ref{tab:re_n16_selection}), consistent with LP being the strongest RE selection signal across scales, though still below greedy at 8B.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation: free-form decoding.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nRemoving grammar constraints (SciERC NER, Qwen3-8B, 4 seeds) yields nearly identical LP distributions (std ratio 0.97) and negligible selection changes (Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), ruling out constrained decoding as the cause of NER compression.\nOn RE, LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding ($+1.8{\\pm}0.1$~pp, 4 seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:freeform_ablation}), while under constrained decoding LP falls below greedy ($-0.7$~pp at 8B).\nRE LP selection is thus regime-dependent: constrained decoding suppresses it, free-form decoding recovers it, and at 4B $N{=}16$ it is marginally positive ($+0.7$~pp, $p{=}0.058$).\nThis pattern is preliminary (single RE benchmark, 40\\% zero-F1 rate).\nVC becomes per-sample in the selection context (each candidate's overlap with the majority-voted output varies), yet tends to select the most typical rather than the best output---consistent with MBR's known mode-seeking bias \\cite{eikema2022sampling}.\nMore broadly, all consensus-proximity strategies fail because utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (Appendix~\\ref{app:mbr_discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of $\\rho$, AUROC, and selection F1 means single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\nEffective selection likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit (Appendix~\\ref{app:n_scaling}).\nStructural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\n",
      "numLines": 25,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 25
    }

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We evaluate five confidence signals for quality estimation and sample selection in LLM-based structured IE, revealing the \textit{correlation-selection gap}: strong quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.45) coexists with selection failure.
4	Instance-level signals fail by construction; the core empirical finding is that log-probability---the only per-sample signal---is too compressed within instances to discriminate (median range 0.041 nats, 60\% tied), and consensus-proximity selection (MBR) fails because utility correlates with F1 across but not within instances.
5	A 4-seed free-form ablation confirms NER compression is not caused by constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary, single benchmark).
6	Signal rankings shift with difficulty across NER benchmarks and collapse at the capability boundary---consistent across two model families and a 4B ablation.
7	RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary, 40\% zero-F1 rate); trained verifiers and scaling to 70B+ are promising directions.
8	
```

```text
645	
646	\section{MBR Decoding and Task-Specific Interactions}\label{app:mbr_discussion}
647	
648	\paragraph{Connection to MBR decoding.}
649	Our selection strategies instantiate minimum Bayes risk (MBR) decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, muller2021understanding}: each selects $\hat{y} = \arg\max_{y_i} \sum_{j \neq i} U(y_i, y_j)$ with utility $U$ defined by SJ, EM, or entity overlap.
650	Despite MBR's success in machine translation \cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here because the utility functions correlate with F1 at the instance level but not within a single instance---the mathematical essence of the correlation-selection gap.
651	\citet{eikema2025structured} study structure-conditional MBR decoding and find that standard utility functions degrade on structured outputs---consistent with our finding that consensus-proximity selection fails in IE despite its success in machine translation.
652	
653	\paragraph{Task-specific decoding interactions.}
654	The free-form ablation reveals divergent behavior across tasks.
655	On RE, LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that spans all 7 relation types and is driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).
656	The win/loss ratio shifts from 36:59 (constrained) to 45:45 (free-form), with the gains concentrated on relation-dense instances (mean 3.3 gold relations).
657	This interaction does not generalize to NER, where selection remains negligible under both regimes.
658	This ablation uses a single seed (seed${=}42$); whether the RE reversal replicates across seeds and generalizes to other RE benchmarks warrants further investigation.
659	The non-tied subset ($n{=}210$) is partially reshuffled under free-form decoding: Jaccard similarity between the constrained and free-form non-tied sets is 0.472, indicating that 87 instances newly gain LP discrimination while others lose it, yielding a $3{\times}$ increase in within-instance LP range for the newly non-tied subset.
660	Despite this reshuffling, aggregate NER selection gains remain negligible---score compression is not a fixed property of specific instances but rather a pervasive regime that shifts across instances without producing net selection improvement.
661	Free-form decoding also slightly improves quality estimation (NER VC $\rho$: 0.379$\to$0.409), though this does not translate to selection gains.
662	
663	\section{Free-Form Decoding Ablation}\label{app:freeform_ablation}
664	
665	Table~\ref{tab:freeform_ablation} compares constrained and free-form decoding across four dimensions on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B; seed${=}42$ shown, 4-seed RE statistics below).
666	Free-form decoding removes grammar constraints entirely; outputs are parsed post-hoc (parse rate 99.95\%, 4406/4408).
667	LP score distributions are nearly identical across regimes (Panel~A: within-instance std ratio $0.97{\times}$), and NER selection gains remain negligible under both regimes (Panel~B).
668	RE LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding across 4 seeds: $+1.84{\pm}0.12$~pp (seeds 42/123/456/789: $+1.69$, $+1.76$, $+2.00$, $+1.91$~pp) vs.\ $-0.68$~pp constrained.
669	Quality estimation improves modestly for most signals under free-form decoding (Panel~C), with the largest gain for FK ($+0.053$ NER, $+0.039$ RE).
670	
671	\begin{table*}[t]
672	\centering
673	\caption{Comparison of constrained vs.\ free-form decoding on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$; RE selection gap replicates across 4 seeds: $+1.84{\pm}0.12$~pp).}
674	\label{tab:freeform_ablation}
675	
676	\vspace{2pt}
677	{\small\textsc{Panel A: Logprob Distribution}}
678	\vspace{2pt}
679

      "content": "\n\\section{MBR Decoding and Task-Specific Interactions}\\label{app:mbr_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to MBR decoding.}\nOur selection strategies instantiate minimum Bayes risk (MBR) decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, muller2021understanding}: each selects $\\hat{y} = \\arg\\max_{y_i} \\sum_{j \\neq i} U(y_i, y_j)$ with utility $U$ defined by SJ, EM, or entity overlap.\nDespite MBR's success in machine translation \\cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \\cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here because the utility functions correlate with F1 at the instance level but not within a single instance---the mathematical essence of the correlation-selection gap.\n\\citet{eikema2025structured} study structure-conditional MBR decoding and find that standard utility functions degrade on structured outputs---consistent with our finding that consensus-proximity selection fails in IE despite its success in machine translation.\n\n\\paragraph{Task-specific decoding interactions.}\nThe free-form ablation reveals divergent behavior across tasks.\nOn RE, LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that spans all 7 relation types and is driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).\nThe win/loss ratio shifts from 36:59 (constrained) to 45:45 (free-form), with the gains concentrated on relation-dense instances (mean 3.3 gold relations).\nThis interaction does not generalize to NER, where selection remains negligible under both regimes.\nThis ablation uses a single seed (seed${=}42$); whether the RE reversal replicates across seeds and generalizes to other RE benchmarks warrants further investigation.\nThe non-tied subset ($n{=}210$) is partially reshuffled under free-form decoding: Jaccard similarity between the constrained and free-form non-tied sets is 0.472, indicating that 87 instances newly gain LP discrimination while others lose it, yielding a $3{\\times}$ increase in within-instance LP range for the newly non-tied subset.\nDespite this reshuffling, aggregate NER selection gains remain negligible---score compression is not a fixed property of specific instances but rather a pervasive regime that shifts across instances without producing net selection improvement.\nFree-form decoding also slightly improves quality estimation (NER VC $\\rho$: 0.379$\\to$0.409), though this does not translate to selection gains.\n\n\\section{Free-Form Decoding Ablation}\\label{app:freeform_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation} compares constrained and free-form decoding across four dimensions on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B; seed${=}42$ shown, 4-seed RE statistics below).\nFree-form decoding removes grammar constraints entirely; outputs are parsed post-hoc (parse rate 99.95\\%, 4406/4408).\nLP score distributions are nearly identical across regimes (Panel~A: within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$), and NER selection gains remain negligible under both regimes (Panel~B).\nRE LP selection consistently reverses direction under free-form decoding across 4 seeds: $+1.84{\\pm}0.12$~pp (seeds 42/123/456/789: $+1.69$, $+1.76$, $+2.00$, $+1.91$~pp) vs.\\ $-0.68$~pp constrained.\nQuality estimation improves modestly for most signals under free-form decoding (Panel~C), with the largest gain for FK ($+0.053$ NER, $+0.039$ RE).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of constrained vs.\\ free-form decoding on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$; RE selection gap replicates across 4 seeds: $+1.84{\\pm}0.12$~pp).}\n\\label{tab:freeform_ablation}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\small\\textsc{Panel A: Logprob Distribution}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & Constrained & Free-form & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean LP std        & 0.0206 & 0.0199 & $-$0.0007 \\\\\nMean LP range      & 0.0623 & 0.0599 & $-$0.0024 \\\\\nTied fraction ($\\varepsilon{=}0.05$) & 59.7\\% & 57.7\\% & $-$2.0\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{8pt}\n\n{\\small\\textsc{Panel B: Selection F1}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n",
      "numLines": 50,
      "startLine": 645,
      "totalLines": 887
    }
  },

747	\section{LP Selection Failure Stability}\label{app:lp_failure_stability}
748	
749	We analyze RE LP selection failures across three random seeds (constrained decoding, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B) to assess whether failures are systematic or stochastic.
750	
751	\paragraph{Pairwise overlap.}
752	LP selection failures (instances where LP-selected F1 $<$ greedy F1) show moderate cross-seed stability: the average pairwise Jaccard similarity is 0.42 (range: 0.38--0.46), indicating that failures are partially systematic---driven by instance complexity---but not fully deterministic.
753	The 3-way intersection contains 34 instances (27.9\% of any-seed failures), confirming a stable failure core.
754	
755	\paragraph{Instance complexity drives failure.}
756	Table~\ref{tab:stable_failures} stratifies instances by failure stability.
757	Stable failures (all 3 seeds) involve more gold relations (2.12 vs.\ 1.62 for never-fail instances) and higher greedy F1 (0.688 vs.\ 0.429), indicating that LP mis-selects on instances where the model already performs well---precisely the instances where selection would be most valuable.
758	Failure rate increases monotonically with gold relation count: 4.3\% at 1 relation, 20.7\% at 3 relations, 26.2\% at 6 relations.
759

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe evaluate five confidence signals for quality estimation and sample selection in LLM-based structured IE, revealing the \\textit{correlation-selection gap}: strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.45) coexists with selection failure.\nInstance-level signals fail by construction; the core empirical finding is that log-probability---the only per-sample signal---is too compressed within instances to discriminate (median range 0.041 nats, 60\\% tied), and consensus-proximity selection (MBR) fails because utility correlates with F1 across but not within instances.\nA 4-seed free-form ablation confirms NER compression is not caused by constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary, single benchmark).\nSignal rankings shift with difficulty across NER benchmarks and collapse at the capability boundary---consistent across two model families and a 4B ablation.\nRE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary, 40\\% zero-F1 rate); trained verifiers and scaling to 70B+ are promising directions.\n",
